**14 / 105** · repo 1 4/45 · repo 2 10/60 · 12 unplanted · 57.0 min · $0.39 · `xhigh` · Codex CLI · single run · 2026-09-23

**Caveat.** ONE RUN, not a mean. This board has measured a 13-point spread between two runs of a single identical max-effort configuration, so treat this as one draw and treat the gap to the rung above or below it as unresolved until it is replicated. Runs a Codex build one version newer than the GPT-5.6 rows here, because the build they use refuses this model; it does compare cleanly to the other GPT-6 rungs. Cost is a list-rate estimate and a floor - the long-context surcharge is not modelled. The machine was idle for both legs.

OpenAI's GPT-6 Luna at xhigh effort through OpenAI's own Codex CLI, on a ChatGPT account - the same auth path as every other Sol, Astra, Terra and Luna row here, NOT the metered API. 14 of 105: 4 of 45 on repo 1 and 10 of 60 on repo 2, 12 genuine extras, 57.0 minutes, $0.39. ONE RUN. *** WHERE THIS SITS *** the GPT-6 Luna ladder currently reads low 4, medium 4, high 9, xhigh 14, max 18.3 (n=3). Its own predecessor's ladder, GPT-5.6 Luna, publishes 31.3 (mean of 3) of 105 at max and 23 at this same rung, which is the generation-over-generation comparison at a fixed dial setting. *** WHY THE SWEEP EXISTS *** a single top-of-ladder number cannot tell a weak model from a mis-set dial. Running every rung of the same model on the same harness does: if the rungs separate, the dial reaches the model and the max figure is the model's ceiling. This family's dial has been steep - the GPT-6 Sol ladder spans roughly five times from low to max. *** ONE RUN IS ONE DRAW *** this board has measured a 13-point spread between two runs of a single identical max-effort configuration. Read any single rung, and any gap between two adjacent rungs, with that width in mind. *** CONFIGURED BYTE-IDENTICAL TO THE MAX RUNG EXCEPT THE EFFORT STRING *** same model string gpt-6-luna, same pinned executable, same code-mode setting, same timeout, same price table, same blind judge. One string differs. *** THIS ROW RUNS A DIFFERENT CLI BUILD FROM THE OLDER OPENAI ROWS, UNAVOIDABLY *** the Codex build the rest of this board uses (0.154.0) refuses this model outright. 0.155.1 runs it on the same account, same auth, same machine, so the blocker was the client and not entitlement. There is no version of this measurement that holds the harness fixed against the GPT-5.6 rows quoted above. The GPT-6 Luna rungs DO compare cleanly to each other and to the GPT-6 Sol rungs - they all run 0.155.1. *** COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE AND A FLOOR *** token counts at OpenAI's published short-context rates, $0.10 input, $0.01 cached, $0.50 output per million - twenty times cheaper than GPT-6 Sol on both sides of the meter. Requests over 272K input bill 2x on input and cache and 1.5x on output, which is NOT modelled here, so any leg that crossed that line cost more than this figure says. The Codex CLI on a subscription exposes no per-run bill, hence `list`, comparable only to other list rows. *** THE JUDGE IS NOT THIS BOARD'S DEFAULT, BY RULE *** a model may never judge itself or a sibling, and the default Codex judge is an OpenAI model, so it is disqualified for this arm - as is every other first-party GPT checkpoint here, since the rule is about shared weights and not identity-knowledge. Judged by grok-4.7, blinded to the arm's identity - the id is read back from the scoring receipt, not the judge config, because the endpoint has resolved a request for one grok checkpoint to another. *** RECEIPTS *** Both legs exited 0 with a report written, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. The machine was otherwise idle for every leg, so the wall figures compare directly against other uncontended rows.

This model's max rung replicated to three runs and landed below all of them, which is why the rungs beneath it are being measured at all: a sweep separates a weak model from a mis-set dial. *** ONE RUN IS ONE DRAW *** this board has measured a 13-point spread between two runs of a single identical max-effort configuration, and this model's own max rung spans three runs. Read any single rung, and any gap between two adjacent rungs, with that width in mind. *** CONFIGURED BYTE-IDENTICAL TO THE MAX RUNG EXCEPT THE EFFORT STRING *** same model string gpt-6-sol, same pinned executable, same timeout, same price table, same blind judge. One string differs. *** THIS ROW RUNS A DIFFERENT CLI BUILD FROM EVERY OTHER OPENAI ROW, UNAVOIDABLY *** the Codex build the rest of this board uses (0.154.0) refuses this model outright - 'The gpt-6-sol model is not supported when using Codex with a ChatGPT account'. 0.155.1 runs it on the same account, same auth, same machine, so the blocker was the client and not entitlement. There is no version of this measurement that holds the harness fixed against the other OpenAI rows. The GPT-6 Sol rungs DO compare cleanly to each other - they all run 0.155.1. *** COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE AND A FLOOR *** token counts at OpenAI's published short-context rates, $2 input, $0.20 cached, $10 output per million. Requests over 272K input bill 2x on input and cache and 1.5x on output, which is NOT modelled here, so any leg that crossed that line cost more than this figure says. The Codex CLI on a subscription exposes no per-run bill, hence `list`, comparable only to other list rows. *** THE JUDGE IS NOT THIS BOARD'S DEFAULT, BY RULE *** a model may never judge itself or a sibling, and the default Codex judge is an OpenAI model, so it is disqualified for this arm - as is every other first-party GPT checkpoint here, since the rule is about shared weights and not identity-knowledge. Judged by grok-4.7, blinded to the arm's identity - the id is read back from the scoring receipt, not the judge config, because the endpoint has resolved a request for one grok checkpoint to another. *** RECEIPTS *** Both legs exited 0 with a report written, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. The machine was otherwise idle for both legs, so these wall figures compare directly against other uncontended rows. (Corrected 2026-09-23: this row first published saying the run shared the laptop, which its receipts do not support - neither leg recorded a peer. The claim was a literal copied from a publish script, and the wall column is the evidence that the effort dial reaches the model at all, so it is worth getting right in both directions.)

## DeepSeek V4-Pro (high effort, DeepSeek API)

**13 / 105** · repo 1 4/45 · repo 2 9/60 · 1 unplanted · 26.6 min · $1.08 bill · `high` · Claude Code / DeepSeek API · single run · 2026-09-10

**Caveat.** Two things to keep in view. (1) The effort label is `first_party`, not `verified`, for the same reason as the max row: a high-vs-max thinking sweep on Pro itself never separated - at n=5 the between-level ratio is 1.35x against a worst within-level spread of 2.27x, with the ranges overlapping heavily. The path-level facts do hold - the endpoint 400-rejects an invented tier and names its enum, and the served model read back as V4-Pro on every gate call. This bench pair is a second and coarser reading of the same question, and it does land in the expected direction (16 at max, 13 at high, with more extras and more money at max) - but it is n=1 per tier on 105 bugs, so it corroborates the dial rather than proving it. (2) This arm was run twice. The first attempt was VOIDED, not scored: a machine-wide tool hook on the bench host pointed at a moved script and denied every tool call the model made, so it diagnosed a broken environment, wrote no report, changed no file, and exited 0 after 160 seconds - a clean-looking row for a leg in which nothing happened. The numbers here come entirely from the re-run after that was fixed, and the runner now voids any leg that produces neither a report nor a single changed file.

The dial companion to the Pro max row: same model, same first-party endpoint, same hour, one tier down. It costs 3 strict fixes - 13/105 against 16 - and saves $0.81, $1.08 against $1.89, on 26.6 minutes against 37.2. It also moves where the score comes from: max is even across the two repos (8/45 and 8/60) while high collapses on repo 1 to 4/45 and edges ahead on repo 2 at 9/60. The unplanted-extras column tracks the money rather than the score - 4 genuine extras at max, 1 at high. high is DeepSeek's documented default; it is requested explicitly here rather than left implicit, because on this endpoint an omitted field measured as neither tier.

At n=3 the means are low 9.7 and medium 13.0 and that ordering reverses: the single medium run was the worst of medium's three. What that n=1 pair supported is therefore narrower than what was published from it. STILL STANDING: medium below high, so the dial does move score at the bottom while the 1-point high-to-xhigh step stays dead in the middle - the margin is 6 points, not the 10 it fired on. RETRACTED: that medium buys 'nearly the same money for less than half the result' (13 against 19 for 9.24 against 10.81), that medium is the worst buy on the board at 1 dollar 11 per fix (the mean is 71 cents), and that medium emits MORE reasoning than high (149,769 against 178,300 - that inversion was one verbose run). *** WHAT THE SPREAD ACTUALLY IS *** Three identical low runs scored 8, 5 and 2 on repo 1. The within-tier spread is larger than the between-tier signal at the bottom of this dial. All six legs were clean: the Linux install returned 0, the shim logged zero errors, every leg exited 0, and no leg carries a handicap note. This is the model at this setting, not the harness. *** AND THE VARIANCE HAS A VISIBLE MECHANISM *** Same arm, same effort flag, repo 1: 221, 202 and 147 tool turns and 69,796, 45,411 and 40,238 reasoning tokens, scoring 8, 5 and 2 - monotone. The effort flag does not fix the amount of work done; the agent varied 1.5x in turns run to run and the score followed. Cost fell with it (3.77, 3.45, 2.83 dollars), so on that leg the cheap run is cheap BECAUSE it did less. SAID PRECISELY: that ordering is clean on repo 1's low legs and NOT on the other three - repo 2's low legs ran 167, 151 and 133 turns for 4, 8 and 2, and medium's most-active repo-1 leg scored lowest of its three. The three replicated rungs produced ranges of 9, 6 and 3 going up the dial. That is an OBSERVATION, not a finding: a range from three draws is a very noisy estimate of spread and three of them cannot establish a trend in variance. Do not read it as 'single runs are safer at the top of a dial'. With the bottom two rungs measured properly the dial is MONOTONE - 9.7, 13.0, 18.7, 20.3, 33.7 - with no dip and no dead middle. The dip was an artifact of n=1 at the two noisiest rungs. Medium costs about 1.6x low and returns about 1.34x the score, so the bottom of this dial is not a bargain: it is cheaper because it does less. Each leg is a full independent run of both repos; the mean is over three, and the per-repo runs are stated so the spread stays visible - it is the finding, not a caveat on it. **Caveat.** One run at each tier, so the 10-point gap is n=1 against n=1 - and this board declined to credit a 6-point gap on the max row for exactly that reason, which is worth explaining rather than glossing. The difference is what else moved. That 6-point gap spanned a change of ROUTE and TIER together with a control arguing against the tier; this one holds route, harness, model, prompt and repos fixed and moves one field by one notch, and it is larger. A repeat at either tier would still turn a strong observation into a finding. WHAT IS NOT CLAIMED: anything about high, which is the rung between these two and is running as this row publishes. Nor that 9 is this model's floor - 2 of 60 on repo 2 is close to the bottom of this board, and a second run at low could land either side of it. billing=LIST ESTIMATE, NOT A BILL: Z.ai publishes no balance or usage endpoint on this plan key, so no credits delta exists to read; costed at the same published Flash rates the OpenRouter Flash row was billed at, which keeps all three Flash rows comparable - and those rates carry a launch discount that can lapse. This endpoint does not report reasoning tokens separately, so output volume is the only work proxy available for the pair. ctx 1M.

The BOTTOM of Z.ai's three-tier dial for this family, run first-party on the same endpoint as the published max row - low, high and max are the entire accept-list here, medium does not exist and cannot be run, and max is also Z.ai's documented default, so low is a tier that has to be asked for and sits BELOW the out-of-the-box run. *** THE DIAL BINDS ON SCORE, AND THIS IS THE FIRST SINGLE-VARIABLE TIER PAIR THIS BOARD HAS RUN ON Z.AI *** 9 of 105 here against 19 at max: same endpoint, same harness, same model, one notch of one field between them. THE TIER IS WORTH TEN POINTS. It shows on the work volume too - 36,777 output tokens against the max run's 115,653, a 3.1x gap - and on the clock, 29.0 minutes against 58.4. *** AND IT ANSWERS A CAUTION THE max ROW PUBLISHED, AGAINST THAT ROW'S READING *** The max row scored 19 against the OpenRouter Flash row's 13 and said in as many words: do not read the jump as an effort result, read 19 as where Flash lands first-party rather than as what max bought. Its reason was the full-size GLM-5.3 control, which scored 19 on BOTH routes. That caution was the honest state of the evidence and the single-variable leg did not exist. It exists now and the dial moves this model by ten points, so the OpenRouter row's 13 sits INSIDE the range the tier alone covers. THE CONTROL IS NOT RETRACTED: the full-size model has no tier pair on this board, only a route pair, so the zero it moved is a statement about its ROUTE and says nothing about its dial, which nobody has run. *** WHERE IT BREAKS IS REPO 2, ALMOST ENTIRELY *** Repo 1 gives up 3 points, 7 of 45 here against 10 at max. Repo 2 nearly collapses: 2 of 60 against 9. The larger and more tangled codebase is where the missing thinking is missed, which is the same place every cheap row on this board loses its points, and it is worth more than the combined number. *** THE PRICE IS HONEST, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE OTHER DIAL PUBLISHED TODAY *** Cost per strict fix: 4.6 cents at low, 5.0 cents at max. You pay 2.2x and you get 2.1x, so the bottom rung is not a value play - it is proportionally less of everything. Compare Muse Spark 1.3 on Meta's endpoint, published hours earlier, where xhigh costs 44% more than high and buys a single point. Two dials, two entirely different value shapes, same method. Honesty profile: 7 of 45 on repo 1 with no partials and no claimed-only, 2 of 60 on repo 2 with no partials and TWO claimed-only, and 4 genuine unplanted extras. Stated flat rather than framed: that is up from one claimed-only on the max row and down from five on the OpenRouter Flash row, so it is inside this model's own range and is not evidence that the bottom rung is less honest.

One string differs. *** THIS ROW RUNS A DIFFERENT CLI BUILD FROM EVERY OTHER OPENAI ROW, UNAVOIDABLY *** the Codex build the rest of this board uses (0.154.0) refuses this model outright - 'The gpt-6-sol model is not supported when using Codex with a ChatGPT account'. 0.155.1 runs it on the same account, same auth, same machine, so the blocker was the client and not entitlement. There is no version of this measurement that holds the harness fixed against the other OpenAI rows. The GPT-6 Sol rungs DO compare cleanly to each other - they all run 0.155.1. *** COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE AND A FLOOR *** token counts at OpenAI's published short-context rates, $2 input, $0.20 cached, $10 output per million. Requests over 272K input bill 2x on input and cache and 1.5x on output, which is NOT modelled here, so any leg that crossed that line cost more than this figure says. The Codex CLI on a subscription exposes no per-run bill, hence `list`, comparable only to other list rows. *** THE JUDGE IS NOT THIS BOARD'S DEFAULT, BY RULE *** a model may never judge itself or a sibling, and the default Codex judge is an OpenAI model, so it is disqualified for this arm - as is every other first-party GPT checkpoint here, since the rule is about shared weights and not identity-knowledge. Judged by grok-4.7, blinded to the arm's identity - the id is read back from the scoring receipt, not the judge config, because the endpoint has resolved a request for one grok checkpoint to another. *** RECEIPTS *** Both legs exited 0 with a report written, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. The machine was otherwise idle for both legs, so these wall figures compare directly against other uncontended rows. (Corrected 2026-09-23: this row first published saying the run shared the laptop, which its receipts do not support - neither leg recorded a peer. The claim was a literal copied from a publish script, and the wall column is the evidence that the effort dial reaches the model at all, so it is worth getting right in both directions.)

## Gemma 4 31B (OpenRouter/Crusoe)

**4 / 105** · repo 1 1/45 · repo 2 3/60 · 2 unplanted · 221.8 min · $8.61 bill · `default` · Claude Code / OpenRouter · single run · 2026-09-16

**Caveat.** ONE RUN PER REPO, not a replicated mean - read the 4/105 as a single data point for this model, not a settled estimate. The repo 1 and repo 2 legs ran under DIFFERENT CONTEXT REGIMES (repo 1 compacted three times, repo 2 zero), so the combined total mixes two conditions rather than two comparable draws of the same one; the per-repo split is the more honest read. NOT FEATURED: mean rows and n=1 rows are not featured by default on this board unless opted in, and this one was not.

First appearance of Gemma 4 31B on this board - native bf16, reached through OpenRouter pinned to one host, Crusoe, fallbacks off (Antigravity does not serve Gemma at all - checked live against 15 models offered, none of them Gemma). 4 of 105 (repo 1: 1, repo 2: 3), 2 genuine unplanted extras (one per repo), and ZERO claimed-only fixes on either repo - nothing it reported went unconfirmed by the blind judge. *** THE TWO LEGS DID NOT RUN IN THE SAME REGIME, AND THIS TIME THE CAUSE IS VERIFIED, NOT JUST FLAGGED *** the repo 1 leg compacted three times, peaking at 198,297 tokens (triggers at 198297, 196796 and 183827) - the repo 2 leg shows zero compactions. That fully accounts for the wall and cost gap between them: repo 1 ran 205.2 minutes for 8.0811 dollars against repo 2's 16.6 minutes for 0.5261 - roughly 12x the wall and 15x the cost for one more repo's worth of the same model on the same day. Repeated auto-compaction means the model spent much of that run re-reading context it had already seen, which is the direct mechanical explanation for the token and cost gap, not model instability or provider throttling. *** MACHINE CONTENTION ALSO TOUCHED THE REPO 1 WALL FIGURE, SEPARATELY *** that leg's clean attempt ran while five other legs shared the box (queued replication work for other arms); contention can inflate wall further but, per this board's standing note, does not touch scores, tokens or cost - the compaction count is the load-bearing explanation for those. A first, EARLIER attempt at this same repo 1 leg ran into the same Luna-adjacent contention window and exited non-zero; it is not this row and cost 0.1943 dollars of its own, separately absorbed, not counted here. BILLING: real OpenRouter bill (credits-delta) via meter=openrouter on both legs, not a list-rate estimate. EFFORT NOT ASSERTED: OpenRouter does not implement a tier field this route honors, so none was requested and none is claimed.

This row SUPERSEDES the single-run row (0/105) published 2026-09-12. Five of the six legs behind this mean have no BUGS_FOUND.md on disk - see the note for why that does not mean unscored: each was judged on its diff directly, and none of the five diffs matched a planted bug either. effort_status stays inert_default: the arm sends no --effort flag, and OpenRouter drops the field this harness would otherwise set, so what runs is Groq's own undeclared default. THE ZERO SURVIVES REPLICATION, AND SO DOES THE REPORT-WRITING FAILURE. Runs scored 0, 0 and 0 of 105 (repo 1: 0, 0, 0 - repo 2: 0, 0, 0). Mean 0.0, range 0. Every one of six legs (three runs, two repos) matched zero of the 105 planted bugs - the single run published 2026-09-12 was not an unlucky draw. *** ONLY ONE OF SIX LEGS ACTUALLY WROTE THE REPORT FILE *** the n=1 note already flagged this on repo 2 (33 turns, five real edits, a full bug report - printed into the transcript instead of written to disk). Replication shows that was not a one-off: five of six legs across both replicates and both repos made real code changes (one to three Edit calls each, every one cleanly terminal_reason=completed) and then printed BUGS_FOUND.md as chat text instead of creating the file. Only the original repo-1 run (2026-09-12) wrote it. Every report-less leg was judged directly on its diff rather than skipped - the diff is ground truth regardless of whether the model also described it (status=completed override, the same method the n=1 abhb leg already used) - and none of the five diffs matched a planted bug; one (the original abhb run) contained one genuine unplanted fix and one cosmetic change, nothing else did. So this is not five unscored legs inflating uncertainty: it is five legs graded the hard way, on code rather than prose, and all five still land at zero. *** REGIME: CANNOT TELL *** this route (OpenRouter -> Groq, via a local shim) carries no Claude Code-native context accounting, so the usual same-model/same-compaction check this board runs before publishing a mean cannot read it - unverified rather than verified, same as every other OpenRouter-routed replicate on this board. Per-leg receipts (repo 1 / repo 2): wall 1.08/2.63, 1.18/2.41 and 1.45/2.57 minutes; cost 0.0169/0.1081, 0.0467/0.0751 and 0.0301/0.0539 dollars, all six REAL OpenRouter credits-delta bills, not estimates. Combined mean cost across all six legs: $0.11. Genuine unplanted extras: 0.3 mean (one across the six legs, on the original abhb run). BILLING: real OpenRouter bill (credits-delta), matching the n=1 row - not a list-rate estimate.

## Opus 5.5 (max effort)

**43 / 105** · repo 1 18/45 · repo 2 25/60 · 9 unplanted · 68.5 min · $60.49 · `max` · Claude Code · single run · 2026-09-22

**Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-23 by the mean of 2 (41.0 of 105). This row reported 43 of 105 from a single run. The replicates are byte-identical re-runs; across 2 the runs span 39 to 43. Nothing here is retracted - it is kept as the receipt for what the board showed before the extra run.

**Caveat.** ONE RUN, not a mean - and this board has already measured a 13-point spread between two runs of one identical max-effort configuration, so treat 43 as a single draw, not a settled level. Runs a 1M context window with zero compactions; every Opus 5 row here runs 200K and compacted, so the gap against them mixes model version with context and is not a clean version comparison. Cost is a list-rate estimate - verified against the harness's own figure to the cent, but not an invoice. The wall figure is contended.

Anthropic's Opus 5.5 at max effort through Claude Code on the first-party API. 43 of 105: 18 of 45 on repo 1 and 25 of 60 on repo 2, 9 genuine extras, 68.5 minutes, $60.49. ONE RUN. The strongest Anthropic result on this board - the Opus 5 sweep peaked at 27 of 105 (max effort) - and level with the best non-OpenAI rows here. *** READ THE CONTEXT WINDOW BEFORE READING THE GAIN - THIS IS NOT A CLEAN VERSION DELTA *** This arm runs the model string claude-opus-5-5[1m] and therefore a 1M context window: it held a peak of 847,590 tokens on repo 2 and crossed ZERO compaction boundaries, carrying both repos whole through every turn. Every Opus 5 row on this board runs a bare slug, which the CLI treats as a 200K window, and those runs compacted repeatedly mid-task. Two variables moved at once - the model version AND how much of the codebase the model could hold while reasoning about it. The honest claim this row supports is 'Opus 5.5 with a 1M window scores 43', not 'Opus 5.5 is 16 points better than Opus 5'. Separating them needs an Opus 5 arm re-run at [1m], which has not been done. *** THE SUFFIX WAS VERIFIED, NOT ASSUMED *** [1m] is not always honoured: on CLI 2.1.272 the string claude-fable-5-1[1m] was recognised and silently normalised to the bare slug, so an earlier arm on this board requested a window it never received. Probed here before any token was spent, on CLI 2.1.280: init.model returns 'claude-opus-5-5[1m]' verbatim, and the running leg's own init record says the same. *** AUTOCOMPACT RAN AT THE CLI DEFAULT, ALSO DELIBERATE *** A user-level CLAUDE_AUTOCOMPACT_PCT_OVERRIDE=30 had been reaching every leg on this machine, which on a 1M window forces a compaction roughly every 300K tokens - the same setting that turned one earlier arm definition into two different machines six weeks apart. It was removed before this run and its absence re-verified in a leg-shaped probe. *** COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE, BUT A VERIFIED ONE *** $60.49 is token counts at Anthropic's published Opus 5.5 rates: $4 input, $20 output, $0.20 cache read - a 0.05x cache multiplier unique to this model, where every other model here uses 0.1x - and $8 cache write, the 1-hour rate, which is what this harness actually writes. That table reproduces the harness's own computed cost to the cent on both legs (delta $0.00000000), so it is confirmed rather than estimated. It is still not an invoice, hence `list`. The row this arm shipped with initially was 2.02x too high because it inherited Opus 5's price table. The audit that caught it then found the smaller version of the same flaw on fifteen already-published Anthropic rows, which assumed a 5-minute cache write and understated themselves by 5 to 32 per cent; all fifteen were corrected the same day, each one verified against its own stream's ephemeral_1h/ephemeral_5m split rather than by rule - one arm really did write 5-minute caches and kept its lower rate. Long context carries no surcharge - Anthropic prices the full 1M window at standard rates. *** RECEIPTS *** Both legs exited 0, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. Judge: blind Codex gpt-5.5 - an OpenAI model scoring an Anthropic one, so neither the model itself nor a sibling of it. The repo 2 judge returned a malformed empty verdict set on its first attempt and was retried once; the retry is the scored result. The run shared the laptop with benchmark legs from another vendor, which the wall marker discloses - scores, tokens and cost are untouched by that, only the clock.

Not an invoice, hence `list`. *** GENUINE EXTRAS *** 9 and 9 across the runs, mean 9.0 - real defects found in the repos that nobody planted, reported separately and never folded into the score. *** THE CONTEXT WINDOW *** every run here holds a 1M window and crosses zero compaction boundaries, so these rows compare cleanly to each other and NOT to the Opus 5 ladder, which ran the CLI's default 200K and compacted repeatedly mid-task. **Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-23 by the mean of 2 (36.0 of 105). This row reported 35 of 105 from a single run. The replicates are byte-identical re-runs; across 2 the runs span 35 to 37. Nothing here is retracted - it is kept as the receipt for what the board showed before the extra run.

This is the xhigh rung of a four-rung same-day sweep (medium 30, high 32, xhigh 35, max 43 of 105); the ladder is monotone, but no gap in it exceeds the 13-point run-to-run spread this board has measured on a single identical configuration, so read the direction, not the ordering of adjacent rungs. Anthropic's Opus 5.5 at xhigh effort through Claude Code on the first-party API. 35 of 105: 16 of 45 on repo 1 and 19 of 60 on repo 2, 14 genuine extras, 43.7 minutes, $35.14. ONE RUN. *** THIS IS ONE RUNG OF A FOUR-RUNG SWEEP *** All four Opus 5.5 rows on this board are the same arm definition with one character changed - the effort dial - run on the same day, on the same machine, against the same two repos, judged the same way. The ladder is monotone: medium 30, high 32, xhigh 35, max 43 of 105, costing $16.55, $23.03, $35.14 and $60.49. *** BUT READ THE LADDER AS A TREND, NOT AS FOUR LEVELS *** every rung is a single run, and this board has already measured a 13-POINT spread between two runs of one identical max-effort configuration elsewhere. No gap on this ladder is that wide - the largest, max over xhigh, is 8. So the direction is credible across four points; the ordering of any two adjacent rungs is not resolved at n=1, and nothing here says xhigh is reliably worse than the rung above it. *** THE DIAL BINDS - MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED *** this board has caught several third-party routes that accept a reasoning field and ignore it, so 'first party' is not taken on trust. Token spend on repo 2 rises monotonically with the dial across the sweep - 33.4M at medium, 46.8M at high, 82.7M at xhigh, 150.2M at max, a 4.5x span - and wall rises with it. This rung burned 82.7M. Whatever else is true, the setting is reaching the model and changing how hard it works. *** THE CONTEXT WINDOW, WHICH IS WHY THESE ROWS DO NOT COMPARE TO THE OPUS 5 LADDER *** this arm runs the model string claude-opus-5-5[1m] and therefore a 1M window: it held a peak of 626,627 tokens on repo 2 and crossed ZERO compaction boundaries, carrying the repo whole through every turn. Every Opus 5 row on this board runs a bare slug, which the CLI treats as 200K, and those runs compacted repeatedly mid-task. Comparing this ladder to the Opus 5 ladder moves two variables at once - model version AND how much of the codebase the model could hold while reasoning about it. The [1m] suffix was verified on CLI 2.1.280 rather than assumed (an earlier arm on this board requested a 1M window and was silently normalised to the bare slug on 2.1.272); init.model returns 'claude-opus-5-5[1m]' verbatim. Autocompact ran at the CLI's own default - a user-level CLAUDE_AUTOCOMPACT_PCT_OVERRIDE=30 that had been reaching legs on this machine was removed before the sweep and its absence re-verified in a leg-shaped probe. *** COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE *** token counts at Anthropic's published Opus 5.5 rates: $4 input, $20 output, $0.20 cache read - a 0.05x cache multiplier unique to this model, where every other model here uses 0.1x - and $8 cache write, the 1-hour rate, which is what this harness actually writes. Not an invoice, hence `list`. *** RECEIPTS *** Both legs exited 0, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. ## Muse Spark 1.3 (max effort, Muse Code, Meta API) - mean of 3

**33.7 / 105** · repo 1 15.3/45 · repo 2 18.3/60 · 26.3 unplanted · 89.8 min · $19.16 · `max` · Muse Code / Meta API · mean of 3 runs · 2026-09-14

**Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-17 by the n=5 MEAN row at the same setting (32.2). Two further runs of the identical configuration scored 29 and 31, so this row's three runs were the top of the distribution rather than the middle of it. Its 33.7 is not wrong and has not been rewritten - it is what three runs of this arm said. What changed is the error bar: range 2 across three runs, range 6 across five. Kept as a receipt precisely because the difference between the two rows is the point.

**Caveat.** Every figure on this row is a MEAN of three runs, carried to one decimal - 33.7 of 105, not a 34 that happens to be an average. The three runs span 33 to 35. This row SUPERSEDES the single-run max row (33/105) published 2026-09-13, which sat 0.7 below the mean; the supersession is about method, not a correction. The two repo figures are rounded independently and sum to 33.6 rather than 33.7 - the combined figure is the mean of the three TOTALS, which is the one that reconciles with the runs; a tenth of a bug is the cost of a fixed precision and is not hidden. THE THREE RUNS DIFFER IN HARNESS VERSION (1.1.1 for the first, 1.2.1 for the other two), which no other replicated rung on this board does - see the note. With this row Meta's dial is the first on this board measured at n=3 on every rung.

THE TOP RUNG OF META'S DIAL, RUN THREE TIMES - and with it, the FIRST EFFORT DIAL ON THIS BOARD REPLICATED END TO END. Runs scored 33, 33 and 35 of 105 (repo 1: 14, 17, 15 - repo 2: 19, 16, 20). Mean 33.7, range 2. *** THE WHOLE DIAL, ALL FIVE RUNGS AT n=3 *** low 9.7, medium 13.0, high 18.7, xhigh 20.3, max 33.7. Monotone, with no dip and no dead middle. Three-run ranges, in the same order: 9, 6, 3, 9, 2. *** WHAT THAT DOES AND DOES NOT LICENSE *** Two adjacent rungs are distinguishable here only where the step clears the run-to-run range at both ends. max over xhigh is a 13.4-point step against ranges of 9 and 2, and is the one gap on this dial that no amount of run-to-run noise explains away. high over medium (5.7 against ranges of 6 and 3) and xhigh over high (under two points against 3 and 9) are NOT separated by anything this board can measure. What is established is the ORDER of the endpoints and the size of the top step; what is not established is that every rung is a rung. *** THE THREE RUNS ARE NOT HARNESS-IDENTICAL, AND THAT IS A REAL ASTERISK *** Run 1 ran Muse Code 1.1.1 (1.1.1-R2514.1); runs 2 and 3 ran 1.2.1 (1.2.1-R2847.1). Muse Code self-updates and did so between waves. Every other replicated rung on this board held the harness version fixed across all three runs, so this rung's mean carries one source of variation the others do not. It is not a reason to discount the row - 1.1.1 scored 33 and the 1.2.1 pair scored 33 and 35, so the update did not move the number in any direction this sample can see - but a mean is a claim that its members differ only by chance, and here they also differ by a version string. Stated so nobody has to reconstruct it from two CSVs. *** WHAT IS SOLID *** Counts, not inferences. A fixed configuration produced 33, 33 and 35 on this rung - the TIGHTEST spread of any rung on this dial, a range of 2 against 9 at the bottom. Across the three runs this rung ever fixed 45 distinct bugs of 105, well above the 33.7 any single run manages: 25 of those 45 fell in all three runs, 6 in two of them and 14 in exactly one. The gap between 'ever' and 'reliably' is the thing a single run cannot show. All six legs were clean: the Linux install returned 0, the shim logged zero errors, every leg exited 0, and no leg carries a handicap note. Per-leg receipts (repo 1 / repo 2): shim calls 471/557, 467/351, 363/484; wall 102.3, 86.6 and 80.4 minutes; cost 19.83, 19.83 and 17.81 dollars. Genuine extras 16, 25 and 38 - a range of 22 on a count that is not scored and is not meant to be, and a useful reminder of how noisy the unplanted column is at n=1. Claimed-only is 0 on five of the six legs; run 2's repo-2 leg claimed 4 fixes the judge did not confirm. Each leg is a full independent run of both repos; the mean is over three, and the per-repo runs are stated so the spread stays visible - it is the finding, not a caveat on it. THE EFFORT LABEL DESCRIBES THE MAIN LOOP, NOT EVERY CALL THIS ROW IS BILLED FOR: Muse Code runs a reminder-observer side agent at fixed efforts the flag does not control. billing=LIST ESTIMATE, NOT A BILL: a subscription account with no per-run credits delta, costed at the same Meta list rates as the other first-party rows, which is what keeps them comparable. ctx 1M.

## Muse Spark 1.3 (max effort, Muse Code, Meta API)

**33 / 105** · repo 1 14/45 · repo 2 19/60 · 16 unplanted · 102.3 min · $19.83 · `max` · Muse Code / Meta API · single run · 2026-09-13

**Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-14 by the n=3 MEAN row at the same rung (33.7). This row is one of three runs that scored 33, 33 and 35; it is kept because a single run is no longer how this rung is reported, and nothing in it is corrected by arithmetic. It is also the only one of the three that ran Muse Code 1.1.1 rather than 1.2.1 - see the mean row's note.

**Caveat.** One run at this tier. *** THIS CAVEAT SAID THE CEILING WAS FLAT AND IT IS NOT - CORRECTED 2026-09-13 *** It was written off the magnitude probe, which could not tell high, xhigh and max apart in thinking volume at n=3 on one fixed prompt. Both rungs below have since been run on the real benchmark: high scores 19 of 105 and xhigh scores 20, against this row's 33. So max is worth about thirteen points over either neighbour and is a purchase that pays on this stack. Superseded wording, kept verbatim rather than deleted: the effort ceiling is real but FLAT, the magnitude probe separates minimal, low and medium into non-overlapping bands but cannot tell high, xhigh and max apart at n=3, so max is the top of the dial and not a rung above high; read the tier as a ceiling that exists rather than as a purchase that pays. What SURVIVES from the probe is the grouping of high with xhigh, which the bench confirms on score as well as on tokens; what failed was extending that group upward to max. THE HARNESS VERSION IS CAPTURED, NOT TYPED: this row records Muse Code 1.1.1-R2514.1, read from muse --version at run time, because muse SELF-UPDATES - it replaced itself between the two legs of the sibling arm on 2026-09-12 and shipped a wrong version string to this board, which is a mistake a hand-written note can make twice and a captured value cannot make once. THE SHIM IN THE PATH IS A METER AND NOT A ROUTE: Muse Code's JSON stream carries no usage or token payloads at all, so without it this row would have no billing record and this board does not publish a cost column it cannot source. Pointed at Meta with the model rewrite and the provider pin both OFF it forwards the turn untouched - no translation of the conversation, the tools, the effort field or the stream - and only counts. It is the same instrument the OpenRouter row used, so the pair differs in ROUTE rather than in apparatus. CREDENTIAL: the subscription credential muse login stores in its own auth file, read at run time rather than copied, so a token muse refreshes cannot leave this arm on a stale one. billing=LIST ESTIMATE, NOT A BILL: a subscription account exposes no per-run credits delta to read, so this is costed from the run's own token counts at the same Meta list rates the OpenRouter row was billed at (1.25 in / 0.15 cache-read / 4.25 out per MTok), which keeps the two Muse Code rows comparable and makes neither a market price. Muse Code's reminder-observer side agent means the turn count and the token bill both include work no Claude Code row on this board does, so the cost column is not comparable to a Claude Code row of the same model even when the model is identical. The benchmark repos were re-installed from the same lockfile inside Linux before the agent started, because the Windows install lays down win32 binaries Linux node cannot execute; repo 2 needed --legacy-peer-deps and the exit code is recorded in the row.

Meta's OWN agent driving Meta's OWN model on Meta's OWN endpoint, at the top of the tier list that endpoint serves - no aggregator anywhere in the path. 33 of 105, which is the best Meta result on this board by a wide margin and nearly DOUBLE the 18/105 the same model and the same harness scored through OpenRouter a day earlier. (33 is the strict-match count this board publishes; the partial-credit figure is 34.5, because repo 2 contributed 3 partials at half each.) *** THE ATTRIBUTION IS NOW SETTLED, AND THIS NOTE ORIGINALLY GUESSED IT WRONG *** It first said the doubling could not be credited to the effort dial, because the route and the tier had moved together and the single-variable leg did not exist. That leg now exists and it runs the other way. Two facts arrived on 2026-09-13: the OpenRouter sibling was found to have run at high rather than at a dropped default, and a first-party leg at high scored 19/105. So the three rows differ one variable at a time - OpenRouter at high 18, Meta first-party at high 19, Meta first-party at max 33 - and THE ROUTE IS WORTH ABOUT A POINT WHILE THE TIER IS WORTH FOURTEEN. The caution was still right to publish: it was the honest state of the evidence, and the GLM-5.3 control it cited (the same move worth zero) remains true of GLM-5.3. Effort dials are not worth the same amount on every stack, which is why this board measures them per path instead of assuming. *** READ THE EFFORT LABEL AS A CLAIM ABOUT THE MAIN LOOP, NOT ABOUT THE RUN *** Receipt: effort-dial-probes/20260912-musecode-cli-effort-vs-wire.txt, which records every request body a run puts on the wire. Muse Code fires a reminder-observer side agent at efforts this flag does not control - fixed low, high and medium, sitting exactly where they were when the flag moved from minimal to max - so on a three-step probe at max, 4 of 10 calls carried max and six did not. The tier below is asserted, validated and echoed FOR THE MAIN LOOP, which is the agent that does the work; it is not true of every call this row is billed for. No other row on this board needed that distinction, because no other harness here runs a second agent. *** max IS THE CEILING, AND ultra IS A TRAP THIS ROW WALKED INTO AND BACKED OUT OF *** Receipt: effort-dial-probes/20260912-musespark13-meta-firstparty-effort.txt. Meta's endpoint validates reasoning.effort in TWO layers - a schema enum, then a per-model support list - and ECHOES the applied tier back inside the response object, which is stronger than any other first-party endpoint here manages: the others prove they READ the field by refusing bad values, this one also reports what it DID with a good one. minimal, low, medium, high, xhigh and max return 200 and echo themselves; none passes the schema and is then refused for this model; ultra and an invented bogus_zzz are refused as unknown variants; a bad model id fails differently again, as a 404. Omitting the field echoes high, so high is the measured default. Muse Code's own help offers ultra, and an earlier version of that receipt concluded a run launched at ultra would have ERRORED rather than run higher. It would not. It runs: the CLI prints that the gate ultra_reasoning_effort is closed and it is using xhigh, exits 0, and puts xhigh on the wire - ONE TIER BELOW max - with nothing in its JSON stream, its exit code or the response to say so. The endpoint never sees ultra because the CLI never sends it. An API's accept-list does not tell you what a CLI sends, any more than a CLI's accept-list tells you what an endpoint serves. *** AND THE DIAL IS NOW MEASURED, NOT JUST VALIDATED - IT BINDS AT THE BOTTOM AND SATURATES AT THE TOP *** Receipt: effort-dial-probes/20260913-musespark13-meta-effort-magnitude.txt, n=3 per tier on one fixed prompt, 18 calls, every call echoing the tier it was asked for. Mean reasoning tokens run 817 (minimal), 2238 (low), 5774 (medium), 9385 (high), 10757 (xhigh), 12655 (max) - a 15.5x bottom-to-top ratio against a 2.19x worst within-tier spread, so the dial is real by this board's magnitude rule. On that toy prompt minimal, low and medium sat in bands that touched nothing while high, xhigh and max overlapped almost entirely, and the receipt concluded max buys nothing measurable over high. *** THE BENCH SAYS OTHERWISE AND THE PROBE WAS THE THING THAT WAS WRONG *** A first-party leg at high scores 19/105 against this row's 33, and on the real workload the tiers separate hard: 178,300 reasoning tokens at high against 286,242 here, a 61% gap the fixed-prompt sweep could not see. The xhigh leg has since run as well and lands at 20, which locates the error precisely: the top of this dial is TWO rungs rather than one, high and xhigh within a point of each other and max thirteen above both, so the probe grouped correctly at high/xhigh and wrongly pulled max in with them. So a token-volume probe shows whether a dial is CONNECTED and not what it is WORTH, and the two must not be used interchangeably - which is also a caution against reading the Qwen3.8 saturation findings as statements about score, since no bench pair was ever run to test them. Honesty profile: 14 of 45 on repo 1 with no partials, 19 of 60 plus 3 partials on repo 2, ZERO claimed-only on either repo across three Meta rows running, and 16 genuine unplanted extras.

## Union Alpha (stealth; free)

**32 / 105** · repo 1 16/45 · repo 2 16/60 · 11 unplanted · 32.7 min · $3.30 · `default` · Claude Code / OpenRouter · single run · 2026-09-17

**Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-17 by the n=3 MEAN row. Nothing here is retracted: the 32 happened and this is its receipt, judged twice and agreed on bug-for-bug. Kept rather than deleted because a supersession is only legible next to the row it supersedes - and this one sat MID-RANGE of the three runs (32, against 34 and 26) yet still above the 30.7 mean, so a reader comparing against 32 was comparing against a slightly lucky draw rather than an outlier.

REVEALED 2026-09-17, hours after this row went up: the stealth slug was unbiased/pareto. OpenRouter announced it directly, and said community detective work cut the stealth period short. The endpoint then returned 404 and every stealth slug left the model list. The row keeps the stealth name because the stealth endpoint is what was measured, and it now carries its real vendor. *** IT TIES GPT-5.6 Terra (max effort) EXACTLY: 16 of 45 and 16 of 60, the same split, for nothing in 32.7 minutes against $27.98 in 159.7. The tie is on the number and not on the work - the two runs share 23 of their 32 bugs, so nine on each side are bugs the other missed. *** PARETO IS NOT A MODEL, IT IS A COMPOSITE - AND THAT IS THE WHOLE STORY OF THIS ROW. Unbiased describes it as running several frontier and open-source models in parallel on every request and selecting or synthesising the output rather than routing to one. That is measurable, and it was measured twice. BEFORE the reveal, from readings taken while the slug was live: a prefix-free doubling probe returned 160 and 200 tokens for one fixed 437-character passage - a 40-token gap where the entire spread across ten different labs on that same passage is 82 tokens, with the hidden prefix cancelled by construction and no special-token markers present for a stripping state to explain. One model has one vocabulary, so this had to be more than one backend. AFTER the reveal, against the live endpoint: the same passage returns 138, 160 AND 200, and 8 of 10 corpus passages come back with two or three different counts. The prediction and the confirmation both hold. WHAT THE WIRE WORK GOT RIGHT AND WRONG: the exclusions were sound as far as they went - Anthropic, xAI, Google and Meta are not the developer - but the question was malformed, because the developer is an aggregator and the weights belong to whoever sits on the panel for that call. An earlier version of this note leaned toward OpenAI on residual evidence. That lean was withdrawn on 2026-09-17, a day before the reveal, and the reveal says it was answering the wrong question rather than answering it wrongly. READ THE TOKEN COLUMNS AS A SELF-REPORT AND NOTHING FIRMER - more so now than when that caveat was written, because the counter behind them belongs to whichever panel member answered: the same fixed string, cache-busted, came back at about 200 prompt tokens with cached_tokens=0 or at 169-172 with cached_tokens=12. THE RUN WAS GENUINELY FREE AND WHAT IT MEASURED WAS EXACTLY ZERO, but the preview has ended: unbiased/pareto now lists at $2.50 per million prompt, $7.50 completion, $0.25 cached read. Repricing this run's own measured token counts at those rates gives $3.30 - against the $27.98 that GPT-5.6 Terra (max effort) actually billed for the identical 16+16 split. So the cell reads $3.30 and is marked cost_list, which the glossary defines as a token-count estimate at published list rates and not a bill. It was $0.00 until the preview ended; an honest zero then stopped supporting the only comparison the column exists for, so the figure was repriced generator-side. Nothing was actually spent, and the published receipts still say so. The comparison survives the preview ending either way, at 8.5x cheaper instead of free. *** THE SLUG SERVED MORE THAN ONE VOCABULARY. *** Predicted before the reveal and confirmed against the revealed endpoint: at least three backends answer to this slug. The benchmark runs themselves do NOT look mixed: the three replicates agree at mean Jaccard 0.628 on their fixed-bug sets against a board median of 0.632 over 24 replicate families, which is ordinary. The probe evidence suggests the router was length-sensitive, so short probes and long agentic runs need not have landed on the same backend. The score is what was measured; that it describes a single model is not something this board can claim. *** THE JUDGE IS NOT THIS BOARD'S DEFAULT, AND WHAT THAT COST WAS MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSERTED. A model may not judge itself or a sibling; with the lab unidentified the routing could not be read off a vendor at all, so the scoring pass ran on grok-4.6. The reveal vindicates that more strongly than the reasoning given for it: a composite has no single vendor to check a judge against, and third-party analysis of the panel places GPT-6 Astra on the escalation path - the default judge's own family, inside the thing being judged. Every other row here was judged by codex, and the measured judge-to-judge spread on one transcript is about 2 points of 105, so this run was ALSO judged by codex into a separate scratch pass that could not redefine the scoring one. The two judges selected the SAME 32 bugs - identical sets on both repos, not merely equal counts. The number does not depend on who read it. *** THERE IS NO EFFORT DIAL ON THIS ROUTE. supported_parameters carries no reasoning field of any kind, the model returns reasoning_tokens=0 with empty reasoning_details on a prompt that makes every reasoning model think, and an invented tier is refused by the gateway in 0.2s without ever reaching the provider. So effort is 'default' and deliberately not 'inert_default': nothing was asserted, and there was no dial to probe. *** ONE RUN, AND THE ROUTE IS VISIBLY NOISY. Six byte-identical temperature-0 calls came back bimodal - about 8s and about 32s, 20 to 218 completion tokens, a 10.9x spread inside one condition. Two replicates are running; when they land, their mean supersedes this row.

## GPT-6 Sol (max effort)

**32 / 105** · repo 1 18/45 · repo 2 14/60 · 48 unplanted · 60.4 min · $10.03 · `max` · Codex CLI · single run · 2026-09-23

**Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-23 by the mean of 2 (31.0 of 105). This row reported 32 of 105 from a single run, published the night the model shipped. The replicates are byte-identical re-runs; across 2 the runs span 30 to 32. Nothing here is retracted - it is kept as the receipt for what the board showed before the extra run.

**Caveat.** ONE RUN, not a mean, on a model measured the night it shipped - and this board has measured a 13-point spread between two runs of a single identical max-effort configuration, so treat 32 as one draw. It also runs a Codex build one version newer than every other OpenAI row here, because the build the others use refuses this model; the harness is therefore not held fixed across that comparison. Cost is a list-rate estimate and a floor - the long-context surcharge is not modelled. The wall figure is contended.

OpenAI's GPT-6 Sol at max effort through OpenAI's own Codex CLI, on a ChatGPT account - the same auth path as every other Sol, Astra, Terra and Luna row here, NOT the metered API. 32 of 105: 18 of 45 on repo 1 and 14 of 60 on repo 2, 48 genuine extras, 60.4 minutes, $10.03. ONE RUN, measured the night the model shipped. *** THE NEWER CHECKPOINT DOES NOT LEAD ITS OWN FAMILY HERE *** GPT-6 Astra publishes 45 of 105 at max (mean of 3) and 43 at xhigh on this same benchmark; GPT-5.6 Sol publishes 43.5 at max (mean of 2). This row lands at 32, below all three, at a fraction of the cost - $10 against GPT-5.6 Sol's $95. A single run cannot settle a gap that size against a mean, but it is a large gap in the direction nobody would have guessed from the release order. *** IT KILLED AN ALL-TIME SURVIVOR *** one repo-1 bug, A6, had outlived every model in every scored run on this board until now - a settings-persistence ordering defect, where a preference was written to storage before the change it described had actually taken effect, so a switch that failed or was dismissed left the stored value and the running session disagreeing. The survivor count drops from 32 to 31. That is worth separating from the score: this row sits in the middle of the table and still opened ground nothing above it had touched. It is also a variance event, not a capability - survivor kills on this board have not reproduced on demand in later runs. *** IT FINDS A LOT OF THINGS NOBODY PLANTED *** 48 genuine extras - real defects in the repos that are not part of the planted set - is among the highest counts on this board, from a row in the middle of the scoring table. Extras are reported separately and never folded into the score, but a model that flags this many unplanted defects while fixing fewer planted ones is spending its attention somewhere the planted total cannot see. *** THIS ROW RUNS A DIFFERENT CLI BUILD FROM EVERY OTHER OPENAI ROW, UNAVOIDABLY *** the Codex build the rest of this board uses (0.154.0) refuses this model outright - 'The gpt-6-sol model is not supported when using Codex with a ChatGPT account'. 0.155.1 runs it on the same account, same auth, same machine, so the blocker was the client and not entitlement. There is no version of this measurement that holds the harness fixed against the other OpenAI rows; the choice was a one-version harness delta or no row at all. Read the comparison above with that in it. (The rejection message is generic - a deliberately fake slug returns it verbatim - so it is not evidence about any model's existence.) *** THE FIRST TWO ATTEMPTS AT THIS ROW WERE VOID, AND THE REASON IS WORTH KNOWING *** the arm was first pinned to npm's shim rather than the real executable. On Windows that shim resolves to a .cmd, running a .cmd makes the shell re-parse the whole argument list, and a NEWLINE ENDS THE COMMAND - so the model received the benchmark prompt's first line, the repo description, and none of the numbered instructions. Both legs launched, billed and exited 0 with nothing to show the harness: one edited code and wrote no report it had never been asked for, the other replied asking what to work on. Nothing in the leg's own output said the prompt was short. Those legs are kept in the ledger, voided and labelled. This row is a clean re-run of both repos afterwards. A third earlier attempt died on an unrelated fault in the CLI's sandbox setup, since fixed. *** COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE AND A FLOOR *** token counts at OpenAI's published short-context rates, $2 input, $0.20 cached, $10 output per million. Requests over 272K input bill 2x on input and cache and 1.5x on output, which is NOT modelled here, so any leg that crossed that line cost more than this figure says. The Codex CLI on a subscription exposes no per-run bill, hence `list`, comparable only to other list rows. *** THE JUDGE IS NOT THIS BOARD'S DEFAULT, BY RULE *** a model may never judge itself or a sibling, and the default Codex judge is an OpenAI model, so it is disqualified for this arm - as is every other first-party GPT checkpoint here, since the rule is about shared weights and not identity-knowledge. Both legs were judged by grok-4.7 (the id is the one the scoring receipt records as SERVED, not the one the judge config asked for - from 2026-09-22 the endpoint has been resolving a request for grok-4.5 to grok-4.7, and notes published before 2026-09-23 named the requested id), blinded to the arm's identity. *** RECEIPTS *** Both legs exited 0 with a report written, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. The run shared the laptop with legs from another vendor, which the wall marker discloses - scores, tokens and cost are untouched by that, only the clock.

## Opus 5.5 (high effort) - mean of 2

**32 / 105** · repo 1 13.5/45 · repo 2 18.5/60 · 9.5 unplanted · 24.1 min · $22.40 · `high` · Claude Code · mean of 2 runs · 2026-09-23

**Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-23 by the mean of 3 (31.7 of 105). This row reported 32.0 of 105 as the mean of 2 runs. The replicates are byte-identical re-runs; across 3 the runs span 31 to 32. Nothing here is retracted - it is kept as the receipt for what the board showed before the extra run.

**Caveat.** Two runs of an identical configuration scoring 32 and 32 of 105 - spread 0. Read the mean with that width, not as a point, and note that two runs cannot bound variance well - the third is still to come. Runs a 1M context window with zero compactions; every Opus 5 row here runs 200K and compacted, so this ladder does not compare cleanly to that one. Cost is a list-rate estimate, not an invoice. Wall figures are contended.

Anthropic's Opus 5.5 at high effort through Claude Code on the first-party API, published as the MEAN OF TWO INDEPENDENT RUNS. Runs scored 32 and 32 of 105 (repo 1: 14, 13 of 45 - repo 2: 18, 19 of 60). Mean 32.0, spread 0. *** THIS SUPERSEDES OPUS 5.5 (HIGH EFFORT) (32 OF 105) *** *** WHY THE REPLICATES EXIST *** the first pass measured this ladder at one run per rung and it came out monotone - medium 30, high 32, xhigh 35, max 43. That was suggestive and nothing more: no gap in it exceeded the 13-point spread this board has measured between two runs of ONE identical max-effort configuration, so the ordering of any two adjacent rungs was unresolved and every note said so. Three runs per rung is what can resolve it, and the whole ladder is being re-measured that way rather than only its top. The board currently reads low 22.3 (n=3), medium 30.3 (n=3), high 31.7 (n=3), xhigh 36 (n=3), max 41.7 (n=3). *** THE REPLICATES ARE BYTE-IDENTICAL TO THE FIRST RUN *** same model string claude-opus-5-5[1m], same effort, same timeout, same price table, same blind judge. Only the arm name differs, so the spread above is run-to-run variance and nothing else. *** WALL AND COST *** Walls 23.8, 24.4 minutes, mean 24.1; cost $23.03, $21.76, mean $22.40. COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE at Anthropic's published Opus 5.5 rates - $4 input, $20 output, $0.20 cache read (a 0.05x cache multiplier unique to this model, where every other model here uses 0.1x) and $8 cache write, the 1-hour rate, which is what this harness actually writes and what the streams confirm. Not an invoice, hence `list`. *** GENUINE EXTRAS *** 7 and 12 across the runs, mean 9.5 - real defects found in the repos that nobody planted, reported separately and never folded into the score. *** THE CONTEXT WINDOW *** every run here holds a 1M window and crosses zero compaction boundaries, so these rows compare cleanly to each other and NOT to the Opus 5 ladder, which ran the CLI's default 200K and compacted repeatedly mid-task. Autocompact ran at the CLI's own default throughout. *** RECEIPTS *** All legs exited 0, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. Judge: blind Codex gpt-5.5 on every leg - an OpenAI model scoring an Anthropic one, so neither the model itself nor a sibling of it. The runs shared the laptop with legs from other vendors, which the wall marker discloses; scores, tokens and cost are untouched by that, only the clock.

## Opus 5.5 (high effort)

**32 / 105** · repo 1 14/45 · repo 2 18/60 · 7 unplanted · 23.8 min · $23.03 · `high` · Claude Code · single run · 2026-09-22

**Superseded.** Superseded 2026-09-23 by the mean of 2 (32.0 of 105). This row reported 32 of 105 from a single run. The replicates are byte-identical re-runs; across 2 the runs span 32 to 32. Nothing here is retracted - it is kept as the receipt for what the board showed before the extra run.

**Caveat.** ONE RUN, not a mean. This is the high rung of a four-rung same-day sweep (medium 30, high 32, xhigh 35, max 43 of 105); the ladder is monotone, but no gap in it exceeds the 13-point run-to-run spread this board has measured on a single identical configuration, so read the direction, not the ordering of adjacent rungs. Runs a 1M context window with zero compactions; every Opus 5 row here runs 200K and compacted, so this ladder does not compare cleanly to that one. Cost is a list-rate estimate, not an invoice. Anthropic's Opus 5.5 at high effort through Claude Code on the first-party API. 32 of 105: 14 of 45 on repo 1 and 18 of 60 on repo 2, 7 genuine extras, 23.8 minutes, $23.03. ONE RUN. *** THIS IS ONE RUNG OF A FOUR-RUNG SWEEP *** All four Opus 5.5 rows on this board are the same arm definition with one character changed - the effort dial - run on the same day, on the same machine, against the same two repos, judged the same way. The ladder is monotone: medium 30, high 32, xhigh 35, max 43 of 105, costing $16.55, $23.03, $35.14 and $60.49. *** BUT READ THE LADDER AS A TREND, NOT AS FOUR LEVELS *** every rung is a single run, and this board has already measured a 13-POINT spread between two runs of one identical max-effort configuration elsewhere. No gap on this ladder is that wide - the largest, max over xhigh, is 8. So the direction is credible across four points; the ordering of any two adjacent rungs is not resolved at n=1, and nothing here says high is reliably worse than the rung above it. *** THE DIAL BINDS - MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED *** this board has caught several third-party routes that accept a reasoning field and ignore it, so 'first party' is not taken on trust. Token spend on repo 2 rises monotonically with the dial across the sweep - 33.4M at medium, 46.8M at high, 82.7M at xhigh, 150.2M at max, a 4.5x span - and wall rises with it. This rung burned 46.8M. Whatever else is true, the setting is reaching the model and changing how hard it works. *** THE CONTEXT WINDOW, WHICH IS WHY THESE ROWS DO NOT COMPARE TO THE OPUS 5 LADDER *** this arm runs the model string claude-opus-5-5[1m] and therefore a 1M window: it held a peak of 482,838 tokens on repo 2 and crossed ZERO compaction boundaries, carrying the repo whole through every turn. Every Opus 5 row on this board runs a bare slug, which the CLI treats as 200K, and those runs compacted repeatedly mid-task. Comparing this ladder to the Opus 5 ladder moves two variables at once - model version AND how much of the codebase the model could hold while reasoning about it. The [1m] suffix was verified on CLI 2.1.280 rather than assumed (an earlier arm on this board requested a 1M window and was silently normalised to the bare slug on 2.1.272); init.model returns 'claude-opus-5-5[1m]' verbatim. Autocompact ran at the CLI's own default - a user-level CLAUDE_AUTOCOMPACT_PCT_OVERRIDE=30 that had been reaching legs on this machine was removed before the sweep and its absence re-verified in a leg-shaped probe. *** COST IS A LIST ESTIMATE *** token counts at Anthropic's published Opus 5.5 rates: $4 input, $20 output, $0.20 cache read - a 0.05x cache multiplier unique to this model, where every other model here uses 0.1x - and $8 cache write, the 1-hour rate, which is what this harness actually writes. Not an invoice, hence `list`. *** RECEIPTS *** Both legs exited 0, run SEQUENTIALLY, one repo at a time. Judge: blind Codex gpt-5.5 - an OpenAI model scoring an Anthropic one, so neither the model itself nor a sibling of it. The run shared the laptop with benchmark legs from another vendor, which the wall marker discloses - scores, tokens and cost are untouched by that, only the clock.

A third independent run - delayed by a Grok judge-bridge fault unrelated to Luna - completed the originally planned n=3, moving the mean to 31.3 across runs of 33, 30 and 31. Nothing here is retracted; it is kept as the receipt for what the board showed before this third run.

**Caveat.** Two runs of an identical configuration scoring 33 and 30 of 105 - n=2, not the n=3 originally planned, because a third replicate's judging failed on a Grok bridge initialization error unrelated to Luna. Cost is a TOKEN ESTIMATE at list rates, not a bill, and is comparable only to other list-priced rows. GPT-5.6 Luna at max effort through OpenAI's own Codex CLI, published as the MEAN OF TWO INDEPENDENT RUNS. Runs scored 33 and 30 of 105 (repo 1: 17, 16 of 45 - repo 2: 16, 14 of 60). Mean 31.5, spread 3. *** THIS SUPERSEDES A SINGLE RUN THAT WAS THE HIGHEST NUMBER ON THIS BOARD *** The earlier row said 33 of 105 from one draw, and it was FEATURED, which makes it the first number a reader meets. That is the most over-read kind of figure a benchmark can publish: nothing about it was false - it said n=1 on its face - but a top-of-board score from a single draw invites a confidence the sample cannot carry. *** WHY n=2 AND NOT n=3 *** A third replicate (gpt56lunamaxr3) was attempted and its two legs ran clean, but judging it failed identically 3 times per bench (`TimeoutError: ACP request timed out: initialize` in the Grok ACP judge bridge, before any real judging began) - a bridge/initialization problem, not a Luna problem, and not resolved before today's benchmarking window closed. This row is published honestly as n=2 rather than waiting on or faking a third value; r3 may be added as a further replicate later if the judge bridge issue is resolved. *** WALL AND COST *** Walls 85.8, 254.3 minutes, mean 170.1; cost $1.80, $3.85, mean $2.83. COST IS A TOKEN ESTIMATE at OpenAI's published rates, not an invoice - the Codex CLI on a subscription does not expose a per-run bill, so this column is `list` and comparable only to other list rows. *** GENUINE EXTRAS *** 31 and 57 across the two runs, mean 44.0 - real defects found in the repos that are not part of the planted set. Not scored, reported because a model that finds bugs nobody planted is doing something the planted total cannot see. *** THE TIER IS FIRST-PARTY AND THAT IS WHY IT CAN BE ASSERTED *** max is Luna's top tier on OpenAI's own endpoint, which validates and separates its effort levels rather than silently clamping an unknown string the way several third-party routes on this board do. *** THE JUDGE IS NOT THIS BOARD'S DEFAULT, BY RULE *** A model may never judge itself or a sibling, and this is an OpenAI model, so the default Codex judge is disqualified. Two legs were judged by grok-4.5 and two by grok-4.7 (the id is the one the scoring receipt records as SERVED, not the one the judge config asked for - from 2026-09-22 the endpoint has been resolving a request for grok-4.5 to grok-4.7, and notes published before 2026-09-23 named the requested id), blinded to the arm's identity. The runs themselves shared the machine with legs from other vendors, which the row's wall marker discloses; scores, tokens and cost are untouched by that - only the clock is. *** RECEIPTS *** All four legs exited 0. Legs SEQUENTIAL within every run.
